                                                   73
10.4    The spin and marked-site estimate
Proposition 10.2 (Marked-grid moment estimate). Say that a diagram fits the q-hook if all
cells belong to its first q rows or its first q columns. Write em (l) = l log(m/l), with em (0) = 0.
Fix θ = 1/2, c0 = 1/100. Consider n = ab ≥ 2, where a, b are powers of two with log2 a, log2 b
differing by at most one, and q ≤ n1/16 a positive integer. Let p ≥ 2 be real and let Ba , Bb ≥ 0.
Suppose that, for each m ∈ {a, b}, every diagram ξ ⊢ m, and every q-hook subdiagram η ⊆ ξ
of size m − l,
                           log Jm (ξ, p) ≤ Bm + θHη + c0 log(m)l − em (l),                      (95)
using −∞ for log zero. Then for every λ ⊢ n and every q-hook subdiagram ω ⊆ λ of size
g = n − t,

 log Jn (λ, p) ≤ θHω + c0 log(n)t − en (t) + aBb + bBa + O t + (a + b)(q 2 + n1/4 ) log(n + 1) , (96)
                                                           (                                 )


The implicit constant is absolute.

    If one grid factor has size 1, balance and n ≥ 2 force n = 2, and the other factor has size
m = n. Apply (95) to that factor with (ξ, η, l) = (λ, ω, t). The allowance in (96) is B2 + 2B1 ,
so the conclusion already holds with zero error because B1 ≥ 0. We may now assume a, b ≥ 2.
    To prove this, in the irreducible λ bound Trλ |CR|p by Trλ XY , where X = |R∗ |p , Y = |C|p ,
by Araki-Lieb-Thirring and cyclic invariance for the trace of powers. These are positive
subgroup elements, each a tensor product. They may each first be split into a sum by
the diagrams ξ on the local symmetric groups (compressions by central subgroup isotypic
projections). Use X, Y also for a fixed resulting pair of terms, still positive group algebra
elements.
    Use an induced module of cell states as follows. There are t distinct labeled holes, one
site each, and the other g sites carry spins with value space V = V+ ⊕ V− (dimensions q, q).
Site permutations act by permuting and using the sign on the odd spins, i.e. a relative-order
sign on the V− factors in the usual graded tensor permutation. Holes are even, without signs.
Equivalently one takes a direct sum over hole positions and plus/minus patterns, transporting
the spin tensors by permutations with that sign. Projection to one placement z of labeled
holes is denoted Dz , leaving free spins. In products computed with reordered site factors the
graded reordering is a unitary identification; all subgroup operations within disjoint blocks
preserve grading and act as usual tensor products after grouping their sites.
    With chosen plus and minus counts and local Schur-Weyl types α, β for the two spin spaces,
spectra on these types correspond to highest weights α, β, both of length ≤ q. For s spins,
the permutation module on this sector consists (apart from the general-linear representation
spaces) of induction from the symmetric-group types α, β ′ , with prime denoting transpose,
by the odd-factor sign. In the presence of l additional labeled holes the whole representation
induces further from Ss to Ss+l .
    Choose a global sector of this kind with plus type µ made of the first q rows of ω and
minus type ν the transpose of the remaining rows. Write Z for its orthogonal projector (full
isotypic under the block-unitary or equivalently general-linear action). The spin module
within g sites contains ω, since cωµ,ν ′ ≥ 1 by the LR rule (the skew part ω/µ just consists of
the lower rows). With the holes the sector thus has λ-multiplicity at least f λ/ω by branching.
Also                                               !
                                                n

                                                 t
by splitting standard tableau fillings into these two cell sets. Let Pλ denote the global
group-type projector.

the sector Z, h[g] has minimum eigenvalue ≥ exp(J), where

                                   J = g H (µi /g)i , (νj /g)j
                                            (                    )

general-linear representation are majorized by its highest weight, so the maximum eigenvalue
of the density power on the sector is bounded by its product of sorted block eigenvalues
raised to highest-weight powers, which is at most exp(−J) by the entropy/product

10.5    Local polynomial dimensions
Some local polynomial factors used below are recalled here. On s ≤ m spins of plus/minus
types α, β, an occurring permutation type η has
                                                  2
                        Dη ≥ (m + q + 1)−O(q ) exp s H(α/s, β/s) .                         (98)
                                                         (                 )


Indeed LR implies ηi ≤ αi + q, ηj′ ≤ βj + q, and η fits the q-hook. For the first inequality a
row of an LR tableau with content β ′ has at most β1′ boxes: match each entry > 1 to a distinct
preceding entry of value one less by the lattice word property. Predecessors must be in strictly
earlier rows, so the chains ending for boxes of this row use distinct ones. Transposition gives
the other inequality. The product of hook lengths off the q × q square in the first q rows is at
                     2 Q
most (s + q + 1)O(q ) αi !: the leg lengths there are at most q, and row portions there have
lengths at most αi . Use the analogous column estimate, and hooks ≤ s on the square. The
hook formula and multinomial estimate now give (98), trivially also for s = 0. Consequently

most |α| Dα Dβ /Dη , and general-linear dimensions satisfy this polynomial-factor bound by
the Weyl formula.

10.6    Twirl the rows before splitting the columns
Split X additionally by projecting orthogonally according to the number li of holes per row i,
and by plus/minus counts and general-linear types of spins there, and symmetric-group type
ηi on the nonhole sites there. For the latter use a direct sum of the spin permutation-type
projectors over hole placements. All local sectors are equivariant under the rows subgroup,
and X preserves them, giving positive terms. Whenever nonzero, ηi is contained in the
previously specified total row type ξi by branching: within each assignment of labels to blocks,
a block’s sector over all placements is induced from its sector of stabilizer representation at
one placement. Before using (97) we perform a twirl decomposition of each positive row-sector
term. For row i with si = b − li > 0 and spin types αi , βi , take a random block-unitarily
conjugated density ρi on V with block spectra αi /si , βi /si (zeros allowed), independent Haar
rotations. The average of its spin tensor power with exponent u per density (using (ρui )⊗si )
acts scalarly on the specified sector with scalar at least
                                        2
                           (n + 1)−O(q ) exp(−u si H(αi /si , βi /si )).


                                                75
This follows by Haar averaging, taking at least the highest-weight eigenvalue divided by
the general-linear dimensions. Indeed the power acts by the general-linear representation,
identically in every multiplicity copy, and its conjugation average over U (q) × U (q) replaces
each general-linear irrep matrix by a scalar by Schur’s lemma. For an empty spin set take
scalar 1 and any density. The row term itself commutes with all such powers in its own row.
Thus it is an average, with normalization the reciprocal scalar, of positive terms obtained by
sandwiching it with the spin powers having exponent u/2. We use the product over rows,
and call the normalized integrand X ′ , so the normalization consists of the product of those
reciprocal scalars. Use positive definite approximations, if needed, at factors tending to 1 in
the estimates by adding small positive scalar matrices to the densities and renormalizing,
then taking limits.
    Choose D = i ρi /a in (97).
                  P


The comparison before the column split. Put A = ZPλ , H = hu[g] , and c = Dω−u , so
that A ≤ cH. At this stage Y is still a positive element of the column group algebra, possibly
split by central total-column types. Thus Y commutes with both A and H. For every positive
row-twirl term X ′ , these commutations give
                                                  (                       )
                 c Tr(X ′ Y H) − Tr(X ′ Y A) = Tr X ′ Y 1/2 (cH − A)Y 1/2 ≥ 0.
The last trace is nonnegative because its two factors are positive. Moreover
                               Tr(X ′ Y H) = Tr(H 1/2 X ′ H 1/2 Y ).
No commutation of X ′ with A or H has been used. We now split Y into its positive local spin
and hole sectors and apply the column twirl. Performing that split before the comparison
would require commutations that its summands need not satisfy.
    Only now split Y by the analogous hole and spin sectors on columns and twirl with
column densities σj at exponent u as above. The right-hand trace needs only the diagonal
compressions of the two terms (row and column) to Dz , summed over placements: a row
operator preserves the row of each hole label, and a column operator its column, so there is
only the original placement as intermediate placement in this trace.

10.7    Positive compression with labeled holes
On a single local block of size m, with l holes at specified sites, write ξ, η, α, β for its total
permutation type, nonhole spin permutation type, and spin general-linear types. Before
inserting h and before the spin-power sandwiching and normalization, the compressed PSD
operator in the selected sector has trace at most
                                                     2   Jm (ξ, p)
                                   (m + q + 1)O(q )                .                         (99)
                                                         (m)l Dη
Here (m)l = m!/(m − l)!. To see this, taking the diagonal with labeled holes fixed retains
only coefficients in the stabilizer Sm−l of those positions from the local group algebra term of
X or Y on type ξ. Here we can identify the local module independently for each assigned
set of hole labels, grouping the sites blockwise using the graded permutation identification.
On fixed placements the action of the retained coefficients is thus via the spin permutation
action in this block. The coefficient restriction remains positive (conditional expectation in
group algebra, or compression of the regular matrix). Its identity coefficient is Jm (ξ, p)/m!,
unchanged, so by regular trace on Sm−l its trace on type η is at most Jm (ξ, p)/((m)l Dη ).
Now apply the local multiplicity bound. All sector compressions here besides the original
total-type restriction use projectors preserving fixed placements and commuting there with
the retained subgroup action.

    For singular densities, replace each ρi and σj by (ρi + εI)/(1 + 2qε) and (σj + εI)/(1 + 2qε).
Keep ε > 0 throughout the twirls and the trace comparison. The bounds above are uniform
in ε; eliminate the inverse density using them before letting ε decrease to zero in the twirl
scalars. This procedure remains valid when the limiting row mean is singular and does not
require an inverse at that limit. All the spin matrices preserve parity, so graded regrouping
conjugates the whole calculation by unitaries and changes neither positivity nor these norms.
    This proves (100).

10.10     Extending the child estimate to an inherited hook
In this subsection Em (ξ; p) = log Jm (ξ, p) and jm (l) = em (l), as in the marked recurrence.
    The parent uses its own hook parameter when invoking the marked recurrence. It is
therefore necessary to extend the child induction from the child’s smaller hook to every hook
inherited from the parent. The extension below in fact applies to every subpartition of the
child diagram.
    Use ϵ, c0 , θ, Qm and Gm from Proposition 10.1.

Lemma 10.3 (Allowance for arbitrary inherited subdiagrams). There are absolute C and
m0 with the following property. Let m ≥ m0 be a power of two and let p ≥ 2. Suppose

           Em (ξ; p) ≤ Gm (ξ)     for large-level ξ,            Em (ξ; p) ≤ 0           for the other ξ.

                                                     78
Then, for every ξ ⊢ m and every subpartition η ⊆ ξ, putting l = m − |η| gives

          Em (ξ; p) ≤ Bm + θHη + c0 l log m − jm (l),           Bm = Cm1−4ϵ log(m + 1).   (101)

This includes every q-hook subpartition, for any inherited q ≥ 1. The constants do not depend
on p or q.

Proof. A zero moment causes no difficulty. If ξ is not large-level, use Hη ≥ 0 and the
elementary minimum

                           min c0 x log m − jm (x) = −e−1 m1−c0 .
                                  (                    )
                          0≤x≤m

Since c0 > 4ϵ, this negative part is bounded in absolute value by the stated Bm . This also
handles the trivial diagram, whose moment is exactly one.
   Suppose next that ξ is large-level. Intersect η with the Qm -hook to obtain α, and let
h = |η| − |α|. Since α ⊆ ξ, the hypothesis gives

                       Em (ξ; p) ≤ θHα + c0 (l + h) log m − jm (l + h).

The derivative jm
                ′ (x) = log(m/x) − 1 is at least −1 on (0, m]. By continuity at zero,


                                      jm (l) − jm (l + h) ≤ h.

Consequently the excess over the claimed expression without Bm is at most

                                  c0 h log m + h − θ(Hη − Hα ).                             (†)

Tableau extension gives Hη ≥ Hα . If h < m1−4ϵ , dropping the last term in (†) proves the
required allowance.
    For larger h, the deleted region is a translated straight partition β: its row lengths are
(ηQm +i − Qm )+ , i ≥ 1. Both its width and its height are at most m/Qm , so every hook length
of β is at most 2m/Qm . The hook formula and h! ≥ (h/e)h imply

                                                       Qm h
                                      log Dβ ≥ h log        .
                                                       2em
Fill α with the first |α| entries of a tableau and fill the translated β with the remaining
entries. The boundary inequalities hold because α is an order ideal in η, giving Dη ≥ Dα Dβ .
Therefore
                                     Qm h        1
                                              [(         )                ]
                  Hη − Hα ≥ h log         ≥h        − 4ϵ log m − log(4e)

for sufficiently large m, when Qm ≥ m1/16 /2. The numerical inequality

                               1

                               16

shows that (†) is nonpositive once m ≥ m0 . This proves the lemma.

    If the two children have different already established exponents, use their maximum p.
Each sweep is a contraction, so increasing the real Schatten exponent only decreases every
Em (ξ; p). Apply the lemma with that common p and then the marked recurrence with the
parent parameter q = Qn . This verifies the all-subdiagram quantifier in the child hypothesis;
restricting it to Qm -hook diagrams would not suffice.



                                                79
10.11    The bounded-exponent induction
Proof of Proposition 10.1. The trivial diagram has moment one and is not large-level. A zero
moment satisfies either branch. Once the finite starting range has been fixed, Lemma 2.2
permits a common exponent large enough for every other diagram in that range, including a
possibly negative Gn (λ). It will also be chosen above the fixed sparse requirement in (94).
The sparse branch then holds whenever that estimate applies; its remaining finitely many
sizes will belong to the starting range. We now fix the large-size thresholds without using
that exponent.
    For larger d, split the dimensions into ⌊d/2⌋, ⌈d/2⌉ yielding a, b. Let p be the maximum
of the child exponents. Lemma 10.3 gives (95) for every parent-hook subdiagram with
Bm = O(m1−4ϵ log(m + 1)). Increasing the child exponent to their maximum is allowed by
contraction. Thus the displayed all-subdiagram hypothesis, including the inherited parent
hook, is satisfied.
    We next check three comparisons needed to apply (96). We may assume the parent is
large-level and has positive moment, so the sparse dimension estimate gives H = Hλ ≥ cn1−ϵ .
    First,
                                        Gn (λ) ≤ 0.6H
for all sufficiently large n. To see this, test the canonical Qn -hook portion α ⊆ λ, and put

                                            1
                                       [(           )              ]
                          H − Hα ≥ s           − 4ϵ log n − log(4e) .
                                            16
Multiplied by θ, this is larger than c0 s log n. Consequently this candidate costs at most θH,
even after its negative deletion entropy is discarded. If s < n1−4ϵ , the candidate costs at most
θH + c0 n1−4ϵ log n = θH + o(H). Both cases prove the asserted bound.
   Second, the deletion count t of an actual minimizing ω satisfies
                                                          H
                                          (                 )
                                   t = O n1−4ϵ +              .

Indeed if t ≥ n1−4ϵ , then log(n/t) ≤ 4ϵ log n and Hω ≥ 0, so

                               (c0 − 4ϵ)t log n ≤ Gn (λ) ≤ 0.6H.

Below that threshold the displayed size bound is immediate.
   Third, use this minimizing ω in (96). The child allowances and the grid error satisfy

                                        aBb + bBa = O(n1−2ϵ log(n + 1)),
                    (a + b)(Q2n + n1/4 ) log(n + 1) = O(n3/4 log(n + 1)).

Together with the bound on t and H ≥ cn1−ϵ , these imply, for an absolute K independent of

                              log Jn (λ, p) ≤ Gn (λ) + KH/d.
For example the first error divided by H/d is O(dn−ϵ log(n + 1)) = o(1); the other sublinear

Choose the size threshold so that K/d ≤ 0.1. The preliminary bound is then at most 0.7H,

                  log Jn (λ, p(1 + A/d)) ≤ Gn (λ) + (K − 0.3A)H/d ≤ Gn (λ).

All thresholds and A are independent of the starting exponent. Choose an integer d0 above
them, also large enough that every child in a recursion step is in the range of the inherited-

of the proof, and set

           pd = P∗    (d ≤ d0 ),    pd = (1 + A/d) max{p⌊d/2⌋ , p⌈d/2⌉ }   (d > d0 ).

Strong induction proves both branches of the proposition. To bound these exponents, follow
a child attaining each maximum until the first index at most d0 . Along this path, parent u
and child v obey u − 1 ≥ 2(v − 1), so the sum of reciprocal bit lengths above the starting
range is at most 2/d0 . Thus pd ≤ P∗ e2A/d0 for every d.

10.12    From moments to forward sweeps
Put P = supd pd < ∞ and choose an integer r ≥ 4P . At large levels, the bound Gn (λ) ≤ 0.6Hλ
and the moment-slack inequality give
                                                      2r
                                             (           )
                          log Jn (λ, 2r) ≤ 1 − 0.4       Hλ ≤ −2Hλ .

Consequently
                                             Jn (λ, 2r) = o(1).
                                     X

                                    λ̸=(n)

The large-level bound is summable with negligible total by the partition count and dimension
bound. At the others use (94) (at most 2k diagrams per sparse k). By Schatten Hölder, the
analogous regular-representation Hilbert-Schmidt sum with Trλ |Bnr |2 in place of Trλ |Bn |2r
is no larger. This sum excluding the trivial diagram is the squared L2 distance of the
permutation density from 1 relative to uniform, by the regular action trace formula. By
Cauchy-Schwarz, rd shuffles therefore suffice in total variation at large d, uniformly over
starting orders by relabeling.


11      Deletion budgets and graded hook overlap
The next method assigns a budget to deleting boxes from a diagram. Deletion trades a large
representation for a smaller diagram inside a thin hook and a collection of named points. A
graded tensor overlap estimate controls the hook; falling factorials account for the points.
The budget first minimizes over every subdiagram and clips the result; the proof then finds a
near-minimizer inside a thin hook.
   Write Wλ = log Dλ . Fix a = 1/4 and b = 10−5 , and set

  En (λ) = min{aWµ + bt log n − t log(n/t)},           t = n − |µ|,   En+ = max(0, En ).   (102)
            µ⊆λ

The t-dependent logarithmic terms have continuous value zero at t = 0. All traces below are
ordinary, unnormalized traces.
Theorem 11.1. There are real exponents p(d) ≥ 1, indexed by integers d ≥ 1, with
supd≥1 p(d) < ∞ such that, for every integer d ≥ 1 and every partition λ ⊢ n = 2d ,
                                                             +
                                   Dλ Tr |Bn |2p(d) ≤ eEn (λ) .

                                                 81
There is a fixed integer d∗ ≥ 1 above which one may take

                         p(d) = (1 + d−1/2 ) max{p(⌊d/2⌋), p(⌈d/2⌉)}.

In particular En+ ≤ aWλ yields a fixed negative power of Dλ for the sweep norm.

11.1    Whitening a graded tensor overlap
Consider an a0 by b0 grid with n = a0 b0 and t deleted cells. Put s = n − t. When s = 0,
the surviving representation is one-dimensional and the overlap bound below is immediate;
thus the whitening argument assumes s > 0. Suppose µ ⊢ s lies in an (r, r) hook, where
1 ≤ r ≤ n1/16 is an integer. Let X, Y be products of positive row and column group-algebra
elements, supported on the local type tuples γ, δ, respectively. Then

 TrVµ XY ≤ TrVγ X TrVδ Y exp{O((a0 +b0 +1)r2 log(n+1)+t)+min(0, Wγ +Wδ −Wµ )}. (103)

Here local dimensions and traces multiply over the lines, so Wγ = i log Dγi and Wδ =
                                                                           P
                                                                                √
   j log Dδj . For the balanced grids used in the induction, a0 + b0 = O( n), the error is
P

O(n4/5 + t). The displayed formula retains the error for arbitrary rectangular geometry.
     We supply the entropy gain in this inequality. Realize the hook inside signed tensors
over Cr ⊕ Cr , with the second summand odd. A compatible compact type has even and odd
weight lists ξ, η. Its entropy h differs from the symmetric group log dimension by O(r2 log n);
carrier dimensions and the number of compatible types cost exp O(r2 log n). Over all grid
lines these errors are O((a0 + b0 + 1)r2 log(n + 1)). The complete positive-factor estimate,
including all carrier multiplicities, is Proposition 5.3, applied with q = r. Its local hook
hypothesis causes no additional restriction here: a local type that occurs in the restriction of
Vµ is a subdiagram of µ by the Littlewood–Richardson rule, and hence is itself an (r, r)-hook
shape. Any other local type acts as zero on Vµ . Thus the proposition proves (103). We retain
the following deformation calculation to explain the entropy gain; the proposition supplies
the passage to arbitrary positive factors and their multiplicities.
     For a local type with list of weights (pi ) and u sites, use the strictly positive den-
sity matrix with spectrum (pi + 1)/(u + 2r). Twirl it over the even and odd unitary
groups. The highest-weight term in the twirl dominates the local type projection with loss
                   2
eWγ +O((a0 +b0 +1)r log(n+1)) , and similarly in the other direction. This regularization permits
inverse square roots even when some weights vanish.
     Work in one compatible global compact sector at a time. For each pair (ξ, η) with at most
r parts in each partition and |ξ| + |η| = s for which Vµ occurs in IndSSs|ξ| ×S|η| (Vξ ⊗ Vηt ), put

                                   Kr = U (r)even × U (r)odd ,

                                   τ = τ(ξ,η) : Kr −→ U (Uτ ).

Here Sξ and Sη denote the irreducible polynomial carrier representations. The action τ extends
polynomially to the invertible parity-preserving block matrices. Write χτ (B) = TrUτ τ (B) for
its character, and
                           h = s log s −     ξi log ξi −   ηj log ηj ,

11.2    The sparse bound and near-optimal deletion
For λ = (n − k, β), a separate path argument gives

                        ∥Bn∗ Bn |Vλ ∥op ≤ eCk (2kd/n)k/2 ,     2kd/n ≤ 1.                  (105)

Apply the detailed weighted-forest proof of (17), with ∆ = 2kd/n. Its inclusion-probability
argument and weighted neighbor bound hold for every k ≤ n/(2d), not only for a fixed
polynomial sparse range. In that proof the sum over forests is

                                                    k j k−j
                                                       ∆ .
                                              X
                                      eCk
                                            1≤j≤k/2
                                                    j!

When ∆ ≤ 1, this is at most eCk ∆k/2 j≥0 k j /j! ≤ e(C+1)k ∆k/2 . Thus the same row-sum
                                          P
                           ′
estimate gives ∥Bn ∥2 ≤ eC k ∆k/2 throughout the full displayed range. Since ∥Bn∗ Bn ∥ = ∥Bn ∥2 ,
this proves (105). For k = 1 the centered kernel is zero, as its one-vertex encounter graph is
isolated; for k = 0 the claimed sparse estimate is unnecessary.
    The budget has the elementary bounds

                            En+ ≤ aWλ ,       0 ≤ En+ − En ≤ Cn1−b .                       (106)

The second follows by minimizing bt log n − t log(n/t) over 0 ≤ t ≤ n. A near-minimizing
diagram can be chosen in the (r, r) hook with r = ⌊n1/16 ⌋, at additional cost O(n99/100 ), and
its deletion size obeys
                           t ≤ n1−b/2 + C(Wλ + n99/100 )/ log n.                          (107)
To prove this carefully, set gn (t) = bt log n − t log(n/t), with gn (0) = 0. For t > 0,

                          gn′ (t) = b log n + log(t/n) + 1 ≤ b log n + 1.

Choose an exact minimizer µ0 ⊆ λ, and let vj be the number of its boxes below row j and
right of column j. They form a translated straight partition νj . Let µ(j) be the diagram left
after deleting that core. Filling µ(j) first and then νj gives Dµ0 ≥ Dµ(j) Dνj . Among the vj
core boxes, the v2j boxes beyond its first j rows and columns have hooks at most 2vj /j; all
other hooks are at most vj . Thus the hook formula and v! ≥ (v/e)v yield

                                  log Dνj ≥ v2j log(j/2) − vj .


                                                84
If v2j ≥ vj /2 > 0, deleting this core changes the objective by at most

                          −a{(vj /2) log(j/2) − vj } + vj (b log n + 1).

For j ≥ ⌈n1/32 ⌉ this is negative at all sufficiently large n, since b − a/64 < 0. Exact minimality
therefore forces v2j < vj /2 whenever vj > 0. Starting at j0 = ⌈n1/32 ⌉ and iterating dyadically
to the largest jh ≤ r gives jh > r/2 and

                             vr ≤ vjh ≤ n2−h ≤ 2nj0 /r = O(n31/32 ).

Truncate µ0 once at r. Its dimension decreases, while the derivative bound makes the
increase of gn at most O(n31/32 log n) = O(n99/100 ). For its deletion size t ≥ n1−b/2 one has
gn (t) ≥ (b/2)t log n. The new objective is at most En (λ) + O(n99/100 ) ≤ aWλ + O(n99/100 ),
and its dimension term is nonnegative. This proves (107). Finally gn has minimum −n1−b /e,
establishing the second bound in (106) even when En is negative.

11.3    The cancellation of named-point factors

    The restriction is positive. Indeed the matrix of regular convolution by X has entries

Each summand is nonnegative. Since |G|/|H| =                i (li )si , it follows that

For factorizable X, coefficient restriction factors over the rows. The column operator Y has
the identical construction with column counts s′j and its own pointwise stabilizer.
    These are exactly the restrictions required by the induced trace. Its diagonal coset blocks
are indexed by ordered positions of the t named points. A product of a row and a column
permutation, in either order, can return a named point (i, j) to itself only if both fix it: the
row permutation changes only the column coordinate, and the column permutation changes
only the row coordinate. Neither can undo a coordinate change made by the other, so the
intermediate position must also be (i, j). This holds for every named point simultaneously.
Consequently, in the diagonal block associated with the position set S, only the pointwise-
stabilizing coefficients of both operators occur. Identifying the other positions with the
carrier of µ gives that block’s trace as TrVµ (XS YS ). The choice of coset representative merely
conjugates this identification. Thus

    Apply (103). Its raw trace bound has gain min(0, U − Wµ ). The child quantities being

    The trivial shape has Dλ = 1 and Bn = I. Its undeleted budget choice has value zero, so

for P > 0. In particular this handles all shapes in Lemma 2.1, without requiring a converse
to that lemma. Every remaining block is nontrivial and has 0 < θd,λ < 1 by Lemma 2.2.

12     A paired level and diagram budget
This proof uses two inductive bounds for the same trace. The level budget pays for tuples
that lose coordinates on restriction. The diagram budget pays for long row and column chains
and for distinct markers outside those chains. Their common interpolation lemma keeps the
finite starting exponent out of all counting errors.
    For p ≥ 1 write Tnλ (p) = Dλ Tr |Bn |2p . When Tnλ (p) = 0, we use the extended-real
convention log Tnλ (p) = −∞. Fix the constants

12.1    Interpolation with a fixed counting exponent
The two-strip argument below uses classical Schatten three-lines interpolation; see [24, Section
3.1, Theorem 3.1]. This reference concerns the interpolation principle, while the profile count
and angle inequality below are proved here.
    Write the sweep as Bn = CR, with R the product of row sweeps and C the product of
column sweeps. Set
                             A = (RR∗ )1/2 ,     B = (C ∗ C)1/2 .
These positive group-algebra operators still factor over their respective lines. Within each
irreducible block of the corresponding row or column subgroup algebra choose unitary
extensions of the polar factors, so that R = AUR and C = UC B. These extensions remain in
their subgroup algebras. Then CR = UC BAUR , and BA has the same singular values as the
sweep. This identifies the positive operators used throughout the interpolation argument.
    Let a representation H contain at least Lλ ≥ 1 copies of Vλ in a global Sn -invariant
subspace S, with the same letter denoting its orthogonal projection. In particular S commutes
with the row and column operators. Suppose the row and column profiles give finite families
{Eα } and {Fβ } of projections on H. Within each family the projections are mutually
orthogonal, and α Eα = β Fβ = IH . Each projection commutes with S; Eα commutes
                  P         P

with the row operator A, and Fβ with the column operator B. Set

                      J = #{(α, β) : Fβ Eα S ̸= 0},        wαβ = ∥Fβ Eα S∥.

                                     Dλ 2p0     2(p −1)

first analytic family is

                                                91
12.2    Small levels by path cancellation
For all sufficiently large d and 1 ≤ k ≤ n3/5 we claim

                                      ∥Bn |Vλ ∥op ≤ e−cs k log n                            (125)

for an absolute cs > 0. We prove this on the injective k-tuple module. Its λ-isotype has
multiplicity Dλ̄ , where λ̄ is the diagram below the first row. Zero extension to all k-tuples,
with uniform product measure, multiplies every squared norm by the same factor (n)k /nk .
An isotype vector has zero average in each separate coordinate: otherwise its averaging would
give a nonzero copy of λ in the (k − 1)-tuple module, contrary to branching.
    Let x, y be independent uniform k-tuples. For I ⊆ [k], let gI (x, y) be n|I| times the
endpoint transition probability for the labels in I, extended by zero when either restricted
tuple is noninjective and then lifted to all coordinates. Set g∅ = 1. On injective x, y, define
a potential contact between two labels as follows. If t is their last differing input bit in
sweep order, their output prefixes of length t − 1 must agree. Their prescribed paths then
enter opposite sides of the same switch at time t. The prescribed routes are incompatible
if their output bits also agree at time t. Otherwise the shared switch saves one of the |I|d
independent coin requirements. It follows that gI = 0 on incompatible routes and gI = 2eI
on compatible routes, where eI counts potential contacts within I. Indeed, the input suffix
and output prefix specify the position before every layer; a collision of prescribed positions
would already give an incompatible potential contact.
    In a bilinear form between the zero-extended isotype vectors, we may replace g[k] by
   I⊆[k] (−1)
P            k−|I| g . Each omitted-coordinate term vanishes by the preceding zero-average
                    I
property. On injective x, y this centered kernel vanishes unless the potential-contact graph
has no isolated vertex: toggle any isolated label in the sum. Its squared Hilbert–Schmidt
norm is consequently at most
                             h                                                        i
                4k max Ex,y 1{x,y injective} 1{every label has a potential contact} gI2 .   (126)
                   I⊆[k]

All expectations in this display use uniform product measure.
    Use one factor of gI as a change of measure. Namely, sample the q = |I| input sites
uniformly without replacement and move them through an independent complete switch field
ω; labels outside I retain independent uniform inputs and outputs. The probability that
the original independent inputs in I were injective is at most one, so it can be discarded
in an upper bound. The remaining factor is 2eI , now counting encounters of the q sampled
actual paths. We may also discard injectivity conditions on outside labels. When their inputs
coincide we define no potential edge between them; this convention agrees with the original
event whenever all inputs are injective.
    Fix ω. Its encounter graph Gω has the n initial sites as vertices; two vertices are adjacent
when their actual paths share a switch. A path has at most one such contact per layer, so
the degree is at most d. Two paths meet at most once: after a meeting they differ in the
edited bit, which is never edited again. For any s vertices there are at most s log2 s/2 induced
edges. To verify this, merge the input suffix classes in a binary tree. At a merge with selected
child counts h, s − h, the new contacts form a matching, of size at most min(h, s − h). The
inequality
                   2 min(h, s − h) ≤ s log2 s − h log2 h − (s − h) log2 (s − h)
telescopes over the tree. Thus a sampled component of size s has weight at most ss/2 .
    For the uniform injective sample of size q ≤ k, let L be the number of sampled vertices in
nonsingleton induced components, let J be the number of these components, and let EI be


                                                   92
the number of sampled induced edges. We prove the precise weighted estimate
                            h                   i                 k 3l/2−2j
                          E 1{L=l,J=j} 2EI ω ≤ eCk log(d+1)                 .                 (127)
                                                                     nl−j
The case l = j = 0 has right-hand side at least one. For j > 0, enumerate ordered disjoint
connected vertex sets of sizes s1 , . . . , sj ≥ 2, with sum l. There are at most 2l size lists. A
connected set of size s can be encoded by a rooted plane spanning tree, a root vertex, and
a neighbor index for each tree edge. There are at most 4s−1 plane tree shapes, so at most
n(4d)s−1 such encodings. An encoding may be redundant; it is used only for an upper bound.
   Assign distinct sample labels to the chosen vertices in at most k l ways. Prescribed distinct
placements of those labels have probability 1/(n)l . On the event L = l, J = j, the actual
                                                                                         Q s /2
components give at least j! ordered witnesses. Each such witness, weighted by h shh ,
bounds 2EI . Hence the expectation is at most

                                2l nj (4d)l−j k l               Y s /2
                                                      max         shh .
                                     j!(n)l       s1 +···+sj =l
                                                                h

For 2 ≤ s ≤ k, ss/2 = s s(s−2)/2 ≤ es k (s−2)/2 , so the product is at most el k (l−2j)/2 . Moreover
(n)l ≥ (n/e)l and 1/j! ≤ eCk k −j , the latter following from j! ≥ (j/e)j and j log(k/j) ≤ k/e.
These bounds prove (127), uniformly in the fixed switch field.
    Put z = log k/ log n ≤ 3/5. The logarithm of the ratio in (127), divided by log n, is

                                (3z/2 − 1)l + (1 − 2z)j ≤ −l/10.

For z ≥ 1/2 both coefficients have the required sign; for z < 1/2, use j ≤ l/2 to obtain the
stronger bound −(1 − z)l/2. Thus l ≥ k/4 supplies a saving of order k log n.
    If l < k/4 and q ≥ k/2, expose the outside endpoints before sampling the q base paths.
Each prescribed outside path has at most d possible base-path neighbors: at every layer
exactly one base path occupies the opposite pre-switch site. Coverage therefore requires at
least h = ⌈k/4⌉ further sampled labels, outside the nonsingleton components, to belong to a
set of at most kd initial sites. In the preceding witness count, choose these labels in at most
2k ways and prescribe their sites in at most (kd)h ways, using 1/(n)l+h for the joint distinct
placements. Since l + h ≤ q ≤ k, this inserts the factor eO(k) (kd/n)h in the same weighted
bound.
    If q < k/2, condition on all sampled trajectories. Let o = k − q. To cover the outside
vertices choose a rooted forest whose components either end at a sampled label or have one
free outside root. Every component of the second kind contains at least two outside vertices,
so there are at most o/2 free roots and at least o/2 forest edges. Root choices cost at most 2o
and each other vertex has at most k parent choices. Expose each parent before its child. For
a fixed contact time, the child’s required input suffix, opposite input bit, and output prefix
jointly have probability 1/n; summing over times gives d/n. Thus, uniformly in the sampled
trajectories, coverage costs at most 2o (kd/n)o/2 for all large d.
    In each case the additional power of kd/n or the bound n−l/10 saves a fixed multiple of
k log n. The costs O(k log(d + 1)), the at most (k + 1)2 choices of l, j, and the 4k in (126)
are smaller than that saving when d is large. The resulting Hilbert–Schmidt bound for the
centered kernel(n)
                  proves (125), after decreasing cs . When k = 1 the centered kernel is zero.
Finally Dλ ≤ k Dλ̄ ≤ nk , so Tnλ (p) ≤ n2k e−2pcs k log n . Choosing a fixed p sufficiently large in
terms of Λ, cs proves the level budget in this range; it also proves the nonnegative diagram
budget there.



                                                    93
12.3    A tuple frame that includes full rows
The sparse estimate settles the small levels. For larger levels we need both the angle and the
trace endpoint in (123). We first construct a frame with total norm cost eCk ; the entropy
saving will come from centering the resulting pieces.
    For definiteness take the first bit batch to have ⌊d/2⌋ bits. Thus the grid has a = 2⌈d/2⌉ rows
and b = 2⌊d/2⌋ columns; put m = b. For larger k, work on the injective k-tuple module. Here
Eα and Fβ are products of the local row and column Specht-isotypic projections, with full shape
profiles α = (µi )i and β = (νj )j , where µi ⊢ b and νj ⊢ a. Let the local levels be si = b − (µi )1
and tj = a − (νj )1 , with totals Js , Jt ≤ k, and put C1 = i si log(b/si ) + j tj log(a/tj ). The
                                                             P                P

required angle estimate is

                               log w ≤ Ck − 14 {k log(n/k) − C1 }+ .                           (128)

We give the full frame construction, including completely occupied rows. Fix the tuple’s
row assignment. In one row let b be the number of sites, u the number of tuple labels, and
s = b − µ1 the local level. Branching requires 0 ≤ s ≤ u ≤ b. Identify its observed labels with
[u] and embed its function isometrically in L2 (Sb ) by completing to b labels and ignoring
those outside [u]. All measures here are uniform probability measures.
    For Z ⊆ [b], let PZ be conditional averaging given the values at labels in Z. This is a
right-subgroup average, commuting with the left action on values. The sum over |Z| = s is
right central. In the regular representation, the whole left µ-isotypic space is Vµ ⊗ Vµ∗ , with
right action on the paired carrier. The invariant dimension for a subgroup permuting the
b − s complementary labels is Dµ̄ by branching, where µ̄ is the lower diagram. Taking the
trace on that right carrier therefore proves

the entries below the first row of a standard tableau. Each PZ f remains in this left type and
is harmonic in its s selected values: a function of fewer selected labels cannot contain a type
of level s.
    Since P[u] f = f , recover the original function by applying P[u] to (129). Put l = |Z \ [u]|
and v = b − u. The only possible indices satisfy

                             0 ≤ l ≤ min(s, v),         |Z ∩ [u]| = s − l.                     (130)

unassigned to one of these values vanishes by harmonicity. The surviving assignments are
exactly the injections φ in the display, each with sign (−1)l . Their number is (u−s+l)l ≤ (u)l .
Every reassigned function has the same norm as PZ f , since its s distinct observed values
have the same uniform injective marginal distribution.

12.4       Conditional collisions and localization
The frame has paid only eCk and has not yet produced the saving in (128). We now use
centering to force collisions among omitted labels, and localize the selected labels using their
row and column counts.
     The vectors in the global level-k type are harmonic after zero extension. Insert the
centering projection Π = kℓ=1 (I − Mℓ ) into their inner product, where Mℓ averages the
                               Q

independent cell of label ℓ. For labels in X ∪ Y , expand this product and let I ⊆ X ∪ Y be
the subset on which the identity factor is chosen. Write the cells supplied to the two functions
as (UℓH , VℓH ) and (UℓG , VℓG ). For ℓ ∈ I these are the same uniform cell. For ℓ ∈ (X ∪ Y ) \ I
they are independent uniform cells. Different labels are independent. In particular a label in
(X ∩ Y ) \ I supplies two independent selected cells; it is not a shared selected cell.
     For each missing label in Z = [k] \ (X ∪ Y ), couple its two choices using main row
and column variables (Uℓ , Vℓ ) and independent alternates (Uℓ′ , Vℓ′ ). The identity choice uses
(Uℓ , Vℓ ) on both sides; the averaging choice uses (Uℓ , Vℓ′ ) in HX and (Uℓ′ , Vℓ ) in GY . The

                                                                    95
raw functions always see the same needed row Uℓ and column Vℓ . Hence toggling a missing
label changes only the two distinctness indicators. Their alternating sum cancels unless
that label has a possible same-cell collision in some choice of side and cell version. Put
M = |Z| and condition first on all nonmissing variables. The raw functions have separated
remaining variables H(U ), G(V ). Whenever the alternating sum is nonzero, the graph of
possible collisions has a forest covering the missing vertices. In every component meeting
nonmissing vertices, grow a forest from all those vertices as separate roots; then each tree has
exactly one nonmissing root. Span each wholly missing component by one tree, which has at
least two vertices and needs one free root. Thus every child exposed along a forest edge is
missing, and there are at least M/2 edges. Choosing parents, sides, and cell versions costs
eO(k) k number of edges . Exposing parent before child, each specified child cell is independent
uniform, so every edge costs 1/n. Uniformly under the conditioning, the coverage probability
is at most eO(k) (k/n)M/2 .
    Let K(U, V ) be the alternating sum after averaging alternate coordinates. Jensen’s
inequality and its 2M summands give E|K|2 ≤ 4M Pr(coverage). It vanishes unless the cells in
O = X ∩ Y ∩ I are distinct. Writing A1 = (EU |H|2 )1/2 and B1 = (EV |G|2 )1/2 , the conditional
Cauchy–Schwarz bound is therefore

                                 eO(k) (k/n)M/4 1{O distinct} A1 B1 .                     (133)

Here the factorization EU,V |H(U )G(V )|2 = A21 B12 is essential: an unconditional probability
estimate alone would not control the weighted functions.
    The dependence of these two weights will also matter in the next step. Put N = X ∪ Y .
After averaging the missing rows, A1 is measurable with respect to

                                  FH = σ (UℓH )ℓ∈N , (VℓH )ℓ∈X .
                                             (                       )


Similarly, after averaging the missing columns, B1 is measurable with respect to

                                  FG = σ (VℓG )ℓ∈N , (UℓG )ℓ∈Y .
                                             (                       )


Only the cells in O = X ∩ Y ∩ I are selected on both sides with a shared value. Given FH ,
the columns (VℓG )ℓ∈Y \O are still independent and uniform; given FG , the rows (UℓH )ℓ∈X\O
have the analogous property. For a label selected on both sides but outside I, this follows
from the independent versions just specified.
    Stratify the nonmissing variables by the row and column counts pi , qj of O, whose size is
o. There are at most (k + 1)a+b = eO(k) strata because k ≥ n3/5 and the grid is balanced.
On a realizable stratum pi ≤ si , qj ≤ tj . For o > 0, the entropy of the uniform measure on its
distinct cells is log o, bounded by the sum of its marginal entropies. Hence

                             pi log(b/pi ) +         qj log(a/qj ) ≥ o log(n/o).
                         X                       X

                         i                       j

Using i pi log(si /pi ) ≤ Js /e and its column analogue, with zero terms interpreted continu-
      P

                        pi log(b/si ) +    qj log(a/tj ) ≥ o log(n/k) − 2k/e.           (134)
                    X                   X

                     i                   j

This is also true when o = 0.
    We make the localization step explicit. In the average of A1 B1 on a stratum, apply

12.6       Closing the level budget
In both inductions, a trivial child has T = 1 and zero level and diagram budgets. If a child
sweep block is zero, its profile has zero trace endpoint and can be discarded. Every remaining
child satisfies the height condition by Lemma 2.1, so the stated induction hypotheses apply
to all child terms retained below.

Figure 2: The chain decomposition, shown schematically. The top a1 rows are taken first; the
column chains contain only the boxes below those rows. The remaining lower-right region
is a translated straight diagram θ. Thus the row chains, lower-column chains, and tail are
disjoint, and their sizes sum to n. The cut is schematic; the asymptotic threshold is defined
in the text.

    Insert the angle (128), the endpoint bound (138), and the profile count into (123). The
remaining task is to compare the sum of child level budgets with the parent budget. Write
δ = log(n/k)/ log n, let ωi = (si /k)(log b/ log n) for row factors and use the corresponding
column weights. For a positive local level si in a block of size mi , put δi = log(mi /si )/ log mi ,
where mi = b on rows and mi = a on columns (with si replaced by ti there). At level zero
set δi = 0; its weight is zero and the trivial child moment is one. Put

12.7    Long chains and the diagram dimension
It remains to treat δ < ϵ, so k ≥ n1−ϵ . For the two moment inequalities we may assume the
parent block is nonzero, since zero moments were handled above. Throughout this subsection
the theorem’s height condition is λ′1 ≤ n/2. Put T = n1−γd and let a1 , b1 be the numbers
of full rows and full columns of λ of length at least T . Split the diagram into all boxes in
its top a1 rows, then all boxes in its first b1 columns below those rows, and the translated
straight diagram θ remaining in the lower right (Figure 2). The first two regions are disjoint
chains, with lengths ηi = λi and ζj = (λ′j − a1 )+ , respectively. Write

   R = |θ|,      M = n − R,         G=                 l log(M/l),          D0 = 1 + a1 + b1 = O(n0.01 ).

The bound on D0 follows from a1 , b1 ≤ n/T = nγd and γd < 0.01. Set G = 0 when M = 0.
   Every tail cell pays the cap vd log n, since both of its full line lengths are below T . In a
top row, replacing the maximum of the full row and column lengths by the row length can

                                                       99
change the contribution only in the first b1 columns, at most a1 b1 cells. This has total cost
O(cd a1 b1 log(en)). Below the top rows, in a long column the maximum is its full column
height. Replacing that height ζj + a1 by its chain length ζj changes the logarithmic sum by
at most
                                   ζj log(1 + a1 /ζj ) ≤ a1
for each nonempty chain. Thus the chain contribution is cd l l log(n/l) up to the same

sum; the two additions can be separated using log(1 + x + y) ≤ log(1 + x) + log(1 + y).

12.8    Color multiplicities and the marked trace
The arm comparison has expressed the parent budget in terms of G and R, and (142) supplies
its dimension scale. To apply (123), we still need the local copy counts, the positive trace
with distinct markers, the child diagram costs, and the color angle. We establish them in
that order.
    Let H be the space of configurations of R distinctly labeled even markers and signed
colors in Ca1 ⊕ Cb1 . Every marker is used exactly once. Thus H is the orthogonal sum, over
the ordered marker-position tuples, of the signed tensor spaces on the remaining M sites.
The compact group K = U (a1 ) × U (b1 ) acts only on colors; zero factors are omitted. The
selected global type is the irreducible representation

                                           π = π(η,ζ) : K −→ U (Uη,ζ ),
                                           Uη,ζ = Sη (Ca1 ) ⊗ Sζ (Cb1 ),

where the carrier factor belonging to a zero group factor is also omitted. Its carrier action
extends polynomially to the invertible parity-preserving block matrices. Take S to be the
full compact π-isotypic projection on H, including all multiplicity copies. No additional
projection is put on the marker labels.
    Indeed, by fixing the even, odd, and marker site pattern and then inducing, Schur–Weyl
duality identifies this color isotype on the permutation side with induction from the Specht
factors Vη , Vζ t , with the markers fixed pointwise. Stacking ζ t below η gives precisely the
unmarked hook subdiagram. Its Littlewood–Richardson coefficient is at least one: the skew
part below η is a translated straight diagram with its row-constant Littlewood–Richardson
tableau. Adding the R ordered markers by branching then gives at least f λ/(unmarked hook) =
Dθ copies of Vλ . The compact carrier dimension can only increase this multiplicity, so in
(123) we may take Lλ = Dθ . Separating the entry sets in the chains and the tail of a standard
tableau gives
                                                M!
                                             !             !
                                  Dλ       n             n G
                                       ≤               ≤    e .                           (143)
                                                h lh !
                                               Q
                                  Lλ      R              R
The last inequality is the elementary multinomial entropy bound.
    In each row include both the local permutation isotype µi and local color isotype (η (i) , ζ (i) )
in Eα ; use (νj , η [j] , ζ [j] ) in each column for Fβ . The superscripts (i) and [j] distinguish
row and column partitions; subscripts denote their parts. Local color projectors commute
with local permutations. They also commute with the global color projection because they
commute with every element of their local color group, including the corresponding factor of
every diagonal global rotation. Thus these projections have all the commutations needed in
(123). Their degrees fix the marker counts

     For clarity, we record the size bounds used in this sum. Every such ρ contains η (i) and
(ζ )t and lies in the (a1 , b1 ) hook. For the latter assertion use a Littlewood–Richardson
  (i)

tableau of ρ/(ζ (i) )t with content η (i) . Beyond column b1 its columns have at most a1 boxes, by
column strictness. Let l0 be the vector of its top-row and lower-column chain lengths, padded
by zeros. Its top counts dominate the parts of η (i) , while the deficit of each lower-column
count from the corresponding part of ζ (i) is at most a1 . Since the two vectors have the same
total Mi , their total absolute discrepancy is at most 2a1 b1 . It follows that

                                   lh0 log(Mi /lh0 ) = Gi + O(D02 log(en)).                                       (145)
                             X

                               h

One may bound the entropy change per changed integer unit by O(log(en)), including the
transition between zero and one.
    The same tableau argument bounds the coefficient in (144). The top a1 rows of ρ contain
at least |η (i) | boxes, and at most a1 b1 of those belong to the base shape (ζ (i) )t . Thus at most
a1 b1 boxes of the skew tableau lie below the top region. In the top region, a weakly increasing
                                                                                     2
row is specified by its counts of the at most a1 letters, giving at most (n + 1)a1 choices. Below
it there are at most max(1, a1 )a1 b1 choices. The number of possible hook shapes ρ is at most
                                                                  2

    The color angle supplies the remaining saving:

              log w ≤ − 21 (G − GH − GV ) + O(R + (a + b)D02 log n),                  w ≤ 1.   (151)

If M = 0, there are no unmarked symbols, G = GH = GV = 0, and (151) follows from w ≤ 1.
Assume M > 0 for the density and deformation argument that follows. We first pass from the
full local color projections to weight projections. Let QH , QV be the products of the specified
local color projections on the rows and columns. Write the additional permutation-type
projections as E perm , F perm , so Eα = QH E perm and Fβ = F perm QV . They commute within
their respective lines and with S. Hence

                    Fβ Eα S = F perm (QV SQH )E perm ,             w ≤ ∥QV SQH ∥.

All three color projections preserve marker positions, so this norm is the maximum of the
norms on fixed marker-position fibers.

Completion of Theorem 12.1. The number J of profile pairs in this application satisfies
                                       (                                 )
                            log J = O n3/4 + (a + b)D0 log(en) .
                                                   √       √
Indeed the permutation partitions contribute O(a b + b a) = O(n3/4 ) to this logarithm.
Every local compact type is specified by at most a1 + b1 nonnegative parts, each at most n,
giving the second term over all lines. Marker counts are fixed by the local color degrees and
introduce no additional profile choices.
    Set ∆G = G − GH − GV . Combining (148), (149), (150), and (151) in (123), then
subtracting (140), gives

                     log Tnλ (p) − Dd (λ) ≤ (1 − c̄)∆G − (p0 − 1)(∆G)+

                                                                             √
To obtain this error, use k ≥ n1−ϵ , D0 = O(n0.01 ), and a + b = O( n). The terms
(a + b)D02 log2 (en), log J, and kn−ϵ log(en) are all o(k/d). The angle is bounded both by its
exponential estimate and by one; therefore its main term is −(p0 − 1)(∆G)+ , with the stated
Op0 error. No factor in this error depends on the final choice of p.
    The first line of the display is nonpositive. If ∆G ≤ 0, use 1 − c̄ > 0; if ∆G > 0, use
1 − c̄ − (p0 − 1) < 0. Let Q = G + R log n. By (142), Q ≥ k/2, and
                                                               log(d + 1)

The balanced parameter increments satisfy cd − c̄ = vd − v̄ ≥ cd−1/3 . Their negative
contribution is at most −cd−1/3 Q and absorbs the error for sufficiently large d. This proves
the dense diagram induction.
   For its comparison with dimension, put Qγ = G + γd R log n. The fixed parameters give
γd ≥ 10/10001 > 0, and γd < 1, so Qγ ≥ cQ ≥ ck. Moreover R = o(Qγ ) uniformly, because
Qγ ≥ γd R log n, and D02 log2 (en) = o(Qγ ) in the dense range. Thus (140) and (142) give

                      Dd (λ) = cd Qγ + o(Qγ ),           log Dλ ≥ Qγ − o(Qγ ).

covered by the level induction. All thresholds are chosen before the finite-base exponent p.
